Reflexive graph lenses in univalent foundations
Jonathan Sterling

Abstract
Martin-Löf’s identity types provide a generic (albeit opaque) notion of identification or “equality” between any two elements of the same type, embodied in a canonical reflexive graph structure ( = A , 𝐫𝐞𝐟𝐥 ) (=_{A},\mathbf{refl}) on any type A A . The miracle of Voevodsky’s univalence principle is that it ensures, for essentially any naturally occurring structure in mathematics, that this the resultant notion of identification is equivalent to the type of isomorphisms in the category of such structures. Characterisations of this kind are not automatic and must be established one-by-one; to this end, several authors have employed reflexive graphs and displayed reflexive graphs to organise the characterisation of identity types. We contribute reflexive graph lenses , a new family of intermediate abstractions lying between families of reflexive graphs and displayed reflexive graphs that simplifies the characterisation of identity types for complex structures. Every reflexive graph lens gives rise to a (more complicated) displayed reflexive graph, and our experience suggests that many naturally occurring displayed reflexive graphs arise in this way. Evidence for the utility of reflexive graph lenses is given by means of several case studies, including the theory of reflexive graphs itself as well as that of polynomial type operators. Finally, we exhibit an equivalence between the type of reflexive graph fibrations and the type of univalent reflexive graph lenses.

New Spaces for Denotational Semantics
; Dr. Tristan Nguyen, Program Manager). Views and opinions expressed are however those of the author only and do not necessarily reflect those of AFOSR.

Conventions

We work in Martin-Löf’s intensional dependent type theory with products
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
, sums
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, identity types
x=_{A}y
, a unit type
\mathbf{1}
, and type
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers; products, sums, and the unit type are assumed to have definitional
\eta
-laws. We do not globally assume any universes, nor function extensionality, nor univalence; these constructs are assumed locally where needed. We use the symbol
\equiv
to denote definitional equality, and
=_{A}
to denote identity types.
We follow the vernacular of univalent foundations, in which
propositions
and
sets
are defined extrinsically in terms of identity types. When we refer to existence, we mean it in the usual propositionally truncated sense of standard mathematical vernacular rather than in the sense of the HoTT Book
(
Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
)
.

1
Introduction

1.1
Martin-Löf’s identity types

In the setting of Martin-Löf’s intensional type theory
(
Nordström et al., 1990
)
, every type
A
comes equipped with a type constructor
x:A,y:A\vdash x=_{A}y:\mathbf{type}
that classifies
identifications
between elements of
A
,
i.e
.
witnesses that two given elements are “equal”. Rather than being defined separately for each
A
, the identity type constructor
=_{A}
is specified generically for all types by means of formation, introduction, elimination, and computation rules. The formation and introduction rule for the identity type

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathbf{type}\quad\Gamma\vdash u,v:A}{\Gamma\vdash u=_{A}v:\mathbf{type}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathbf{type}\quad\Gamma\vdash u:A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{refl}:u=_{A}u}

can be thought of as equipping
A
with the structure of a
reflexive graph
1
1

1

In the context of category theory, one uses the term “graph” to refer to what combinatorists would normally refer to as
multigraphs
or
pseudo-graphs
. A graph in the traditional sense would be a graph (in the present generalised sense) in which the type of vertices is a set and the type of edges between two fixed vertices is a proposition; we might refer to such objects as “locally propositional set-graphs”.
(see
Definition

9
). The elimination and computation rules

\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathbf{type}\quad\Gamma,x:A,y:A,p:x=_{A}y\vdash C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbf{type}\quad\quad\Gamma,x:A\vdash c\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,x,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\quad\Gamma\vdash u,v:A\quad\Gamma\vdash w:u=_{A}v}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{J}_{A}^{C}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c;u,v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}:C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u,v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash A:\mathbf{type}\quad\Gamma,x:A,y:A,p:x=_{A}y\vdash C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbf{type}\quad\Gamma,x:A\vdash c\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,x,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\quad\Gamma\vdash u:A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{J}_{A}^{C}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle c;u,u,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv c\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u$}\mathclose{}{)}}:C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u,u,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}

then ensure that this reflexive graph structure is the smallest possible one that can be formed with vertices in
A
. This universal property is most commonly employed in terms of the principle of
identification induction
below.

Principle 1

(Identification induction)
.

Given a family of types
x,y:A;p:x=_{A}y\vdash C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbf{type}
, to define a dependent function
x,y:A;p:x=_{A}y\vdash f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}:C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
it suffices to specify
x:A\vdash f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,x,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:C\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,x,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

There is an alternative, equivalent, formulation of identification induction due to
Paulin-Mohring (1993)
, that fixes an element
u:A
and allows induction on data of the form
y:A,p:u=y
. This is called “based identification induction”:

Principle 2

(Based identification induction)
.

Given a fixed element
u:A
and a family of types
y:A,p:u=_{A}y\vdash B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbf{type}
, to define a dependent function
y:A,p:u=_{A}y\vdash f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}:B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
it suffices to specify
f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u,\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Independently,
van den Berg and Garner (2011)
and
Lumsdaine (2010)
have shown that identification induction exhibits a weak globular
\infty
-groupoid structure on each type
A
. In the lowest dimension, this corresponds to the construction of compositors for identifications and associators for the resulting compositions,
etc
.

1.2
Characterising identity types using (displayed) reflexive graphs

The role of the identity type is similar to that of equality in set theory: it expresses a single
global
notion of identification whose rules apply to all types simultaneously.
2
2

2

Naturally, intensional type theory can speak about more than sets — but the spirit of having a single global notion of identity is the same in both type theory and set theory.
On the other hand, because the rules of identity types have to apply uniformly to all types, these rules do not reflect any of the specific characteristics of individual types that would, if taken seriously, make it easier to both exhibit and use identifications.

The goal of reflexive graphs is to provide a methodology
within
Martin-Löf’s type theory by which the special properties of identifications in different types can be made explicit. An alternative design would be to use a type theory in which the identity types are not defined uniformly in each type, and instead each type comes with its own identity type, as in
Observational Type Theory

(
Altenkirch and McBride, 2006
;
Altenkirch et al., 2007
)
and
Higher Observational Type Theory

(
Altenkirch et al., 2024
)
. Each design has trade-offs, but in this paper we are restricting attention to what can be done without changing the type theory.

Remark 1
.

Everything in this section is derived from prior works, such as the HoTT Book
(
Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
)
, Rijke’s textbook on homotopy type theory
(
Rijke, 2025
)
, and the work of
Schipp von Branitz and
Buchholtz (2021)
, although we do impose our own terminological conventions.

1.2.1
Shallow characterisation of identity types

For example, it is very natural to build up an identification of type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{A\times B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}
from a pair of identifications
x=_{A}y
and
u=_{B}v
. This principle can be formalised by
characterising
the identity type of
A\times B
in terms of the identity types of
A
and
B
.

Proposition 2

(Shallow characterisation of the binary product)
.

Each of the following functions is an equivalence:

\displaystyle x,y:A;u,v:B

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{splitId}_{A,B}\colon:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{A\times B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u=_{B}v$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle x:A,u:B

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{splitId}_{A,B}~\mathbf{refl}:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbf{refl},\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Proof.

We can explicitly construct an inverse to
\mathsf{splitId}_{A,B}
as follows:

\displaystyle x,y:A;u,v:B

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{unsplitId}_{A,B}\colon:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u=_{B}v$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{A\times B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle x:A,u:V

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{unsplitId}_{A,B}\,\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbf{refl},\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\equiv\mathbf{refl}

The coherences of the equivalence are given by identification induction.
∎

Of course, although the characterisation of identifications in
A\times B
in
Proposition

2
is useful, it does not help us directly to characterise identifications in more complex types. For example, we may wish to characterise the identity type of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\times B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times C
by asserting that the canonical map
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}},m$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\times B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times C}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}},n$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u=_{B}v$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle m=_{C}n$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence. This can be established directly in the same way as
Proposition

2
or by making use of the lemma, but the latter saves very little time. We would prefer to have a toolkit for building up “deep” characterisations of identity types piece-by-piece.

1.2.2
Deep characterisation using reflexive graphs

A “deep” characterisation of the identity type for binary products would take the following form:

If we have characterised the identity type of
A
and the identity type of
B
, then we may characterise the identity type of
A\times B
.

Proposition

2
can be “deepened” along these lines by replacing the types
A
and
B
with
reflexive graphs

\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
so that we have the following data:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\mathbf{type}

{\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}x

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\mathbf{type}

{\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}}x

These reflexive graph structures follow the pattern of instances of the formation and introduction rules for the identity type. Tentatively, we shall say that a given reflexive graph
\mathcal{G}
is
univalent
when the following function is an equivalence:
3
3

3

See
Definition

35
; in fact, our provisional definition of the univalence condition for a reflexive graph is not the most practical one for everyday use, but it is one of several equivalent conditions that we shall expose later.

\displaystyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{G}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{idToEdge}_{\mathcal{G}}^{x,y}:x=_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{G}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}}y\to x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{G}}}y

\displaystyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{G}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{idToEdge}_{\mathcal{G}}^{x,x}\mathbf{refl}:\equiv\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{G}}{x}

We shall often refer to a univalent reflexive graph as a
path object
for short. With these constructions on reflexive graphs in hand, a
deep
characterisation of the identity type for binary products can be formulated.

First we define a new reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
as follows:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}}v$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x},\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}{u}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Then the deep characterisation of binary products amounts to saying that path objects (
i.e
.
univalent reflexive graphs) are closed under binary products.

Proposition 3

(Deep characterisation of the binary product)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are two path objects, then
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
is a path object.

The compositional character of
Proposition

3
allows us to deduce other useful corollaries immediately by iteration. For example, if
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
are all univalent reflexive graphs, then
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathcal{C}
is a univalent reflexive graph,
etc
. On the other hand, the shallow characterisation (
Proposition

2
) is immediately recoverable via the following definition of the
discrete
reflexive graph on a type
A
, which is univalent by definition:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\triangle}{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle:\equiv A

\displaystyle x\mathrel{\approx_{{\triangle}{A}}}y

\displaystyle:\equiv x=_{A}y

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{{\triangle}{A}}{x}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathbf{refl}

Then
Proposition

2
is precisely the instantiation of
Proposition

3
with
\mathcal{A}:\equiv{\triangle}{A}
and
\mathcal{B}:\equiv{\triangle}{B}
.
We can also generalise the binary product of reflexive graphs to arbitrary arity: if
A
is a type and
\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a reflexive graph for each
x:A
, define
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
to be the following reflexive graph:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\lvert}{\big\lvert}{\big\lvert}{\lvert}}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rvert}{\big\rvert}{\big\rvert}{\rvert}}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle f\mathrel{\approx_{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}g

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}fx\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}gx

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}{f}

\displaystyle:\equiv\lambda{x}\mathpunct{.}\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle fx$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Proposition 4

(Deep characterisation of product)
.

Given
\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
a path object for each
x:A
, the product
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is univalent assuming dependent function extensionality holds.

1.2.3
Dependent types and displayed reflexive graphs

So far we have described how to give shallow and deep characterisations of
non-dependent
types. Of course, most mathematical structures of any interest (
e.g
.
monoids, groups, rings,
etc
.
, or even reflexive graphs themselves!) are described by dependent types, as the types of the operations depend on the carrier types.

Given a type
A:\mathbf{type}
and a family of types
x:A\vdash B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbf{type}
, how can we characterise the identity type of the sum
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
? A shallow characterisation in the style of
Section

1.2.1
is at least not out of reach.

Proposition 5

(Shallow characterisation of the indexed sum)
.

Each of the following functions is an equivalence:

x,y:A;u:B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}},v:B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\vdash

\mathsf{dsplitId}_{A,B}\colon\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}p_{*}^{B}u=_{B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}v

\mathsf{dsplitId}_{A,B}\,\mathbf{refl}:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathbf{refl},\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

The shallow characterisation in
Proposition

5
is more complex than that of
Proposition

2
because the second component of the the sum
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}p_{*}^{B}u=_{B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}v
involves a
transport
. From a higher vantage point, the problem being solved by transport here is that we wish to identify
u:B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
with
v:B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
but these do not have the same type. Transport uses the identification
p:x=_{A}y
to find a common type in which
u
and
v
can be compared; but note that the choice of a forward transport
p_{*}^{B}\colon B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is somewhat arbitrary, as we might have also defined a backward transport
p^{*}_{B}\colon B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and compared
u,v
in
B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

In order to generalise
Proposition

5
to a
deep
characterisation of the indexed sum, we must naturally generalise the concept of reflexive graph to one in which vertices form a
dependent
type and edges go between different components linked by an edge in the base. This is precisely the purpose of the
displayed reflexive graphs
of
Schipp von Branitz and
Buchholtz (2021)
.

In particular, let
\mathcal{A}
be a reflexive graph as before and let
\mathcal{B}
be a
displayed reflexive graph
over
\mathcal{A}
so that we have the following data:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\mathbf{type}

{\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}x

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

{\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathbf{type}

\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}}u

Any displayed reflexive graph such as
\mathcal{B}
gives rise to a family of ordinary reflexive graphs
\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
indexed in vertices
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
.

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}v

\displaystyle:\equiv u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}}v

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}{u}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{x}{u}

A displayed reflexive graph
\mathcal{B}
will be called
univalent
(or a displayed path object for short) when each of its components is univalent. Considering only the components for univalence may seem at first counterintuitive, but this definition is justified by
Proposition

6
below. For this, we first define the
total reflexive graph
of
\mathcal{B}
with vertices in the sum of
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
over
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}.\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}.\mathcal{B}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}v

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}.\mathcal{B}}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x},\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{x}{u}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Then the deep characterisation for indexed sums is exactly the statement that the total reflexive graph of a displayed path object is a path object.

Proposition 6

(Deep characterisation of the indexed sum)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a displayed path object over a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
. Then the total reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}.\mathcal{B}
is a path object.

1.3
Characterising
transport
with reflexive graph lenses

Everything we have described so far is more or less standard. Path objects (and equivalent concepts such as
torsorial families
) have been used to great effect in a variety of formalised libraries of univalent mathematics, including
agda-unimath

(
Rijke et al., 2023
)
and the 1Lab
(
1Lab Development Team, 2022
)
. The purpose of the present paper is to give a deeper analysis of a large class of displayed path objects that arise in a particularly simple way, in fact obviating the need to separately prove many univalence lemmas. We return to the shallow characterisation of the identity types of dependent sums from
Proposition

5
:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}\cong\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x=_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}p_{*}^{B}u=_{B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}v

In our deep characterisation (
Proposition

6
), we generalised
x=_{A}y
to the edges
x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y
of a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
and we generalised
p_{*}^{B}u=_{B\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}v
to the
displayed edges

u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}{v}
of a displayed reflexive graph
\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{A}
. This generalisation abstracts away the use of transport to get the vertices
u
and
v
to lie in the same component of
B
, at the cost of needing to specify
\mathcal{B}
as a displayed reflexive graph rather than as a family of reflexive graphs indexed in
\mathcal{A}
.

Our starting point is to consider a different, less abstract, generalisation of
Proposition

5
in which we replace
A
with a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
as before, but we replace
B
not with a displayed reflexive graph over
\mathcal{A}
, but instead with a
family
of reflexive graphs
\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
indexed in vertices
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
that is equipped with a
transport
or
pushforward
operation to jump from one component to the next. To a first approximation, we have assumed the following data:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\mathbf{type}

{\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}x

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

{\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\mathbf{type}

\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{)}}}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}}u

\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

With this data in hand, we can define an appropriate displayed reflexive graph
\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
over
\mathcal{A}
that mirrors the passage from the shallow characterisation (
Proposition

5
) to the deep characterisation (
Proposition

6
):

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}}^{p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y}}v

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}{u}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}v

It remains to define the displayed reflexivity datum
\mathsf{rx}_{\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}}^{x}{u}:\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}{u}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}u
, but we do not have anything on hand from which to define this. We are therefore led to assert this reflexivity datum as part of the
data
of
\mathcal{B}
:

\mathsf{pushRx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}{u}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}u

The above has the appearance of an
oplax unitor
for the pushforward operation. Using this oplax unitor, we may finish defining the displayed reflexive graph
\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}}^{x}{u}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathsf{pushRx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{x}{u}

Altogether, we shall refer to a family of reflexive graphs
\mathcal{B}
equipped with
\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}
and
\mathsf{pushRx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}
as an
oplax covariant lens
of reflexive graphs (see
Definition

54
) over
\mathcal{A}
—
covariant
because
\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}
implements a forward transport, and
oplax
because of the orientation of the unitor
\mathsf{pushRx}_{\mathcal{B}}
. The terminology of
lenses
is borrowed from
Johnson and Rosebrugh (2013)
;
Chollet et al. (2022)
, who use it to refer to an algebraic generalisation of fibrations in which the chosen lifts are not required to have a universal property but instead satisfy a unit law (strictly, in the case of
op. cit
.
).

Naturally, we can (and will) introduce a dual notion of
lax contravariant lens
for characterising backward transport in a family of reflexive graphs. When
\mathcal{B}
is a lax contravariant lens in this sense (see
Definition

55
), we can likewise associate a displayed reflexive graph
\mathsf{disp}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
in which a displayed edge
u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{disp}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}}^{p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y}}v
is given by an edge
u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{pull}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}{v}
.

Proposition 7

(See
Lemma

60
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an oplax covariant (
resp
.
lax contravariant) lens of path objects over a reflexive graph
\mathcal{A}
. Then the displayed reflexive graph
\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
(
resp
.

\mathsf{disp}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
) is univalent.

The pay-off of introducing lenses of reflexive graphs is twofold. First of all, many naturally occurring displayed reflexive graphs have the shape of
\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
or
\mathsf{disp}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
already; more importantly, however, it frequently happens that the components of the given displayed reflexive graph arise as a pre-existing family of reflexive graphs for which we have already proved univalence. Therefore, it is advantageous to obtain a displayed path object automatically from a very simple algebraic structure on a pre-existing family of path objects: a pushforward operator and a lax unitor.

1.4
Characterising
identification induction
with dependent lenses

Naturally, not all useful displayed path objects arise from (oplax covariant, lax contravariant) lenses. We have found, however, that many of the important counterexamples instead arise from a common generalisation of oplax covariant and lax contravariant lenses, which shares the advantages thereof over working directly with displayed reflexive graphs.
Consider for example the case of
magmas
in a universe
U
, which are specified by the following data:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\mathbf{type}

\otimes_{M}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

An equivalence of magmas
M\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{Magma}_{U}}}N
is given by an equivalence of types
f:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\cong\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
that preserves the binary operation in the following sense:

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
f\times f
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
\otimes_{M}
f
\otimes_{N}

If we write
\mathcal{U}
for the reflexive graph structure on the universe
U
given by equivalences of types, then we would expect that the reflexive graph
\mathsf{Magma}_{U}
should arise as the total reflexive graph of the following displayed reflexive graph over
\mathcal{U}
:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv A\times A\to A

\displaystyle{\otimes_{A}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}}^{f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B}}{\otimes_{B}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x\otimes_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle fx\otimes_{B}fy$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}}^{A}{\otimes_{A}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\lambda{x,y}\mathpunct{.}\mathbf{refl}

The displayed reflexive graph above does
not
arise from an oplax covariant lens, nor from a lax contravariant lens. The following somewhat artificial displayed reflexive graphs could have been obtained from such lenses, but they would be difficult to use:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{+}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv A\times A\to A

\displaystyle{\otimes_{A}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{+}}^{f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B}}{\otimes_{B}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f^{-1}x\otimes_{A}f^{-1}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}=_{B}x\otimes_{B}y

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{+}}^{A}{\otimes_{A}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\lambda{x,y}\mathpunct{.}\mathbf{refl}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{-}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle:\equiv A\times A\to A

\displaystyle{\otimes_{A}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{-}}^{f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B}}{\otimes_{B}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y:A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}x\otimes_{A}y=_{A}f^{-1}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle fx\otimes_{B}fy$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{+}}^{A}{\otimes_{A}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\lambda{x,y}\mathpunct{.}\mathbf{refl}

In order to capture the structure necessary to obtain the displayed reflexive graph
\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}
from a
family
of ordinary reflexive graphs, we must consider a different kind of lens. Before generalising, we first work with this specific case. Let
\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}
be a reflexive graph as defined below for each equivalence
f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B
of types:

\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\equiv\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\triangle}{B}

Then we define the following operations on
\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}
:

\mathsf{lext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{\bullet}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{U}}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\mathsf{lext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B}{\otimes_{A}}:\equiv\lambda{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}:A\times A}\mathpunct{.}f\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x\otimes_{A}y$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\mathsf{rext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{\bullet}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,B:U$}\mathclose{}{\}}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{U}}{B}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\mathsf{rext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B}{\otimes_{B}}:\equiv\lambda{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}:A\times A}\mathpunct{.}fx\otimes_{B}fy

\mathsf{extRx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{\bullet}:\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:U$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:U$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:U$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A:U$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\otimes_{A}:A\times A\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\otimes_{A}:A\times A\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\otimes_{A}:A\times A\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\otimes_{A}:A\times A\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{lext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{U}}{A}}{\otimes_{A}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{U}}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{rext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{U}}{A}}{\otimes_{A}}

\mathsf{extRx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{A}{\otimes_{A}}:\equiv\mathsf{rx}_{\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\_:A\times A$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\triangle}{B}}{\otimes_{A}}

We can now reconstruct the displayed reflexive graph
\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}
in terms of
\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{U}}{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle{\otimes_{A}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}}^{f:A\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{U}}}B}}{\otimes_{B}}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{lext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{f}{\otimes_{A}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{rext}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{f}{\otimes_{B}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}}^{A}{\otimes_{A}}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{extRx}_{\mathsf{BinOp}_{U}^{\pm}}^{A}{\otimes_{A}}

Abstracting from the specific example of binary operations, the critical move above has been (1) to replace a given family of reflexive graphs
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\vdash\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
with a more general family
x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}};p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y\vdash\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}

such that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,x,\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, and then (2) define coercions
\mathsf{lext}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}:\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
\mathsf{rext}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}:\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x,y,p$}\mathclose{}{)}}
from the “left-hand” and “right-hand” diagonal components to the “centre”. Naturally these coercions must be further equipped with at least a coherence of the form
\mathsf{extRx}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{x}u:\mathsf{lext}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{rext}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}u
in order to generate a reflexivity datum; it will happen that in order for the univalence property carry over from
\mathcal{B}
to the associated displayed reflexive graph, we shall also need a coherence of the form
\mathsf{rextRx}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{x}{u}:u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\mathsf{rext}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}{u}
. Together, all this data forms what we shall refer to as a
unbiased dependent lens
of reflexive graphs over
\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 8
.

In the same way as oplax covariant (
resp
.
lax contravariant) lenses express the interface of forward (
resp
.
backward)
transport
for a family of reflexive graphs, unbiased dependent lenses express the interface of (forward and backward)
based identification induction
: the coercions
\mathsf{lext}
and
\mathsf{rext}
extend data defined on a given reflexivity datum
\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}
to data defined on an arbitrary path based at
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
.

1.5
Discussion of related work

1.5.1
Path objects in homotopy type theory

A biased version of path objects was introduced already in the HoTT Book
(
Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
, § 5.8)
under the name
identity system
; this concept is developed much further in the displayed direction by Rijke in his introductory textbook on homotopy type theory
(
Rijke, 2025
, § 11.2)
. In their guise as
torsorial families
, biased identity systems are employed pervasively in
agda-unimath
, an “online encyclopedia of formalized mathematics … from a univalent point of view”
(
Rijke et al., 2023
)
. An unbiased version of identity systems is used extensively in the 1Lab
(
1Lab Development Team, 2022
)
, another library of univalent mathematics formalised in Cubical Agda
(
Vezzosi et al., 2019
)
.

Our work is most directly inspired by that of
Schipp von Branitz and
Buchholtz (2021)
;
Schipp von Branitz (2020)
, who have emphasised the importance of displayed path objects as an organising principle for univalent mathematics. The various notions of lens that we introduce can be thought of as organisational devices to simplify carrying out the methods suggested by
op. cit
.
in practice.

1.5.2
Reflexive graphs and reflexive graph fibrations

Although
univalent
reflexive graphs are the ones that are important for characterising identity types, we have found it very useful to develop as much structure as possible in the language of ordinary reflexive graphs; this is because the reflexive graph structure captures the aspects of an identity type’s characterisation that we intend to have good definitional/computational behavior, whereas the univalent part describes a universal property that we expect to hold (opaquely) only up to homotopy. Our starting point in studying the theory of reflexive graphs has been the doctoral dissertation of
Rijke (2019)
, who introduces many important concepts from the univalent point of view, including the univalence condition (called
discreteness
by
op. cit
.
) as well as the notion of (covariant, contravariant) fibration of reflexive graphs. (It should be noted that the concept of fibred (non-reflexive) graph was introduced already by
Boldi and Vigna (2002)
.)

Schipp von Branitz and
Buchholtz (2021)
;
Schipp von Branitz (2020)
introduced
displayed reflexive graphs
as a more type theoretical presentation of homomorphisms of reflexive graphs; we have re-based
Rijke (2019)
’s theory of reflexive graph fibrations in terms of displayed reflexive graphs in roughly the same way that
Ahrens and Lumsdaine (2019)
re-examine (Street) fibrations of categories in terms of displayed categories.

1.5.3
The structure identity principle

Coquand and Danielsson (2013)
have famously observed that Voevodsky’s univalence principle ensures that the identity types of various algebraic structures (
e.g
.
groups, rings, graphs,
etc
.
) can be characterised so as to coincide precisely with the natural notion of invertible homomorphism: for example, an identification of groups valued in a univalent universe is precisely a group isomorphism. Results of this kind are called
structure identity principles
; a convenient general form of the structure identity principle for concrete categories appears in the HoTT Book
(
Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
, § 9.8)
; a simpler version was introduced by
Escardó (2022)
for his lectures at the Midlands Graduate School in 2019, and included as part of the
TypeTopology
and
cubical
Agda libraries
(
Escardó and contributors, 2024
;
The Agda Community, 2024
)
.

These structure identity principles are the special case of the more general theory of path objects, restricted to the case of displaying an algebraic structure over the path object given by types and equivalences. Of course, not all important path objects take this form: for example, it is necessary to characterise the identity type of the type of sections of a given map, and the base of this thing is a function space rather than a universe. This is why it is important to develop the theory of path objects in its most general form, and then specialise it to obtain a variety of structure identity principles.

1.5.4
Delta lenses and applied category theory

Diskin et al. (2011)
have introduced
delta lenses
as an algebraic structure modeling bidirectional transformations between systems (modelled as categories). As many authors have pointed out
(
Chollet et al., 2022
;
Johnson and Rosebrugh, 2013
;
Clarke, 2020
)
, delta lenses can be thought of as an algebraic generalisation of split opfibrations in which lifts are chosen functorially but are not required to be cocartesian.

Our reflexive graph lenses resemble a version of delta lenses that replaces categories with reflexive graphs, but there are some important differences. When
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:\equiv\mathsf{disp}^{+}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}
is the displayed reflexive graph associated to an oplax covariant lens
\mathcal{B}
, we do indeed assign to each
u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and
p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y
a displayed vertex
\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}{u}:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and a lift
\bar{p}:u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}^{p}}\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}{u}
given by the reflexivity datum on the former in
\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. In contrast to delta lenses, we do
not
require that
\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}u
is equal to
u
: we require only an
oplax unitor
,
i.e
.
an edge
\mathsf{pushRx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{x}{u}:\mathsf{push}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}{u}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}u
.

We also consider contravariant versions of reflexive graph lenses, which turn out to be equally important in practice. Our alignment of (covariant, contravariant) and (oplax, lax) is not chosen arbitrarily, but is rather forced by the need to transform a lens of whichever variance into a displayed reflexive graph.

In
Section

3.4
, we study an important class of reflexive graph lenses in which pushforward along the reflexivity edge is definitionally equivalent to the identity function. These
definitional lenses
correspond most closely to delta lenses, and arise frequently when establishing structure identity principles in the sense of
Section

1.5.3
.

1.6
The structure of this paper

In
Section

2
, we shall recall the theory of (displayed) reflexive graphs and path objects in detail (at the risk of some repetition). In
Section

3
, we expose the theory of (oplax covariant, lax contravariant, and unbiased dependent) lenses and prove the uniqueness of all these structures on families of path objects over a path object. In
Section

4
, we develop a case study that applies the theory of reflexive graph lenses to characterise the identity types of reflexive graphs, displayed reflexive graphs,
etc
.
— in short, we construct large reflexive graphs classifying small reflexive graphs,
etc
. Finally, in
Section

5
, we develop the theory of reflexive graph fibrations (building on
Rijke (2019)
) and conclude with our main result: an equivalence between reflexive graph fibrations and lenses of path objects.

2
Reflexive graphs and path objects

We recall the theory of reflexive graphs from
Rijke (2019)
, incorporating the viewpoint of
displayed
reflexive graphs from
Schipp von Branitz and
Buchholtz (2021)
.

Definition 9

(Reflexive graph)
.

A
reflexive graph

\mathcal{A}\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}},\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is defined to be a type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}:\mathbf{type}
of
vertices
together with a family of types
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\vdash x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y
of
edges
and a reflexivity datum
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\vdash\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}x
.

At times we may refer to
homomorphisms
of reflexive graphs.

Definition 10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be reflexive graphs. A
homomorphism

f\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is given by the following data:

\displaystyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle\vdash\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}(x):\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle x,y:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}};p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y

\displaystyle\vdash f^{\approx}_{x,y}(p)\colon\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}(x)\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}(y)

\displaystyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle\vdash f^{\approx}_{x,x}(\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}x)=_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}x}\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}x)

Definition 11

(Displayed reflexive graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a reflexive graph. A
displayed reflexive graph

\mathcal{B}\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}},\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\bullet}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
over
\mathcal{A}
is given by the following data:

\displaystyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle\vdash\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbf{type}

(vertices)

\displaystyle p:x\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}y,u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}},v:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\vdash u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}v:\mathbf{type}

(edges)

\displaystyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}},u:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\vdash\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{x}{u}:u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}}u

(reflexivity)

Definition 12

(Components of a displayed reflexive graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a displayed reflexive graph over
\mathcal{A}
. Then the
component
of
\mathcal{B}
at an element
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
is the reflexive graph
\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
defined from
\mathcal{B}
as follows:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}v

\displaystyle:\equiv u\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{A}}{x}}}v

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}{u}

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathsf{rx}_{\mathcal{B}}^{x}{u}

Notation 13

(Diagonal family of reflexive graphs)
.

When
\mathcal{B}
is a displayed reflexive graph over
\mathcal{A}
, we will at times write
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{rx}}
for the “diagonal”
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
-indexed family of reflexive graphs assigning to each
x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
the component
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{rx}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\equiv\mathcal{B}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

2.1
Basic constructions on reflexive graphs

The most basic reflexive graph structure is the
discrete
one provided by its identity type.

Definition 14

(Discrete reflexive graph)
.

The
discrete reflexive graph

{\triangle}{A}
on a type
A
is given by its identity type,
i.e
.
we define
{\triangle}{A}:\equiv\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A,=_{A},\mathbf{refl}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

On the other extreme, we may consider the
codiscrete
reflexive graph structure.

Definition 15

(Codiscrete reflexive graph)
.

A type
A
can be, conversely, equipped with the
codiscrete reflexive graph
structure
{\triangledown}{A}
that identifies any two elements:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\triangledown}{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}

\displaystyle:\equiv A

\displaystyle x\mathrel{\approx_{{\triangledown}{A}}}y

\displaystyle:\equiv\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rx}_{{\triangledown}{A}}{x}

\displaystyle:\equiv*

Definition 16

(Total reflexive graph)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a reflexive graph and
\mathcal{B}
is a displayed reflexive graph over
\mathcal{A}
, then the
total reflexive graph

\mathcal{A}.\mathcal{B}
can be defined with vertices in
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{A}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathcal{B}$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
and edges
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a_{0},b_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}.\mathcal{B}}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle a_{1},b_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
in
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:a_{0}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:a_{0}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:a_{0}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle p:a_{0}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{A}}}a_{1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}}}b_{0}\mathrel{\approx_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}}b_{1}
, with reflexivity data given by
